Asymptotics of the Empirical Bootstrap Method Beyond Asymptotic Normality
Morgane Austern & Vasilis Syrgkanis

Abstract
One of the most commonly used methods for forming confidence intervals for statistical inference is the empirical bootstrap, which is especially expedient when the limiting distribution of the estimator is unknown. However, despite its ubiquitous role, its theoretical properties are still not well understood for non-asymptotically normal estimators. In this paper, under stability conditions, we establish the limiting distribution of the empirical bootstrap estimator, derive tight conditions for it to be asymptotically consistent, and quantify the speed of convergence. Moreover, we propose three alternative ways to use the bootstrap method to build confidence intervals with coverage guarantees. Finally, we illustrate the generality and tightness of our results by a series of examples, including uniform confidence bands, two-sample kernel tests, minmax stochastic programs and the empirical risk of stacked estimators.



1
Introduction

One of the most important tasks in statistical inference is to draw confidence intervals for the mean of a given estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}:=g_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
. When the limiting distribution of
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is known, such knowledge can be leveraged to build confidence intervals that are asymptotically consistent. For example,
if
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is asymptotically normal and if
\hat{s}_{n}
is a consistent estimator of its standard deviation, then the following interval is an asymptotically consistent confidence interval at level
1-\alpha
:

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}P\left(\theta\in\Big[\hat{\theta}_{n}-\Phi^{-1}(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\frac{\hat{s}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}},\hat{\theta}_{n}+\Phi^{-1}(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\frac{\hat{s}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big]\right)=1-\alpha.

However, in general the limiting distribution of the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is unknown and an alternative approach must be taken. One such approach is the empirical bootstrap method, which consists of: sampling new observations
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}
independently and uniformly in
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
, and defining
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{{\rm boot}}
as the value of the estimator taken at the bootstrap sample
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{{\rm boot}}:=g_{n}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n})
. This procedure can be repeated many times in order to estimate the conditional distribution of
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{{\rm boot}}-\mathbb{E}(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{{\rm boot}}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
. If this distribution is approximately the same as the distribution of
\hat{\theta}_{n}-\mathbb{E}(\hat{\theta}_{n})
, as the sample size
n
grows, we say that the bootstrap method is consistent. We note that when this holds the bootstrap method can be used to establish approximate confidence intervals for
\mathbb{E}(\hat{\theta}_{n})
. Notably, when
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is asymptotically normal those intervals are known to be consistent under general conditions; see e.g.
[
28
,
5
,
15
]
.

As the bootstrap method is often used for estimators whose limiting distribution is unknown or non-Gaussian, we are interested in studying the limiting distribution of
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{{\rm boot}}
for a general class of estimators with arbitrary limiting distributions and that satisfy simple stability conditions. In particular, we assume that the functions
(g_{n})
are approximable by three-times differentiable functions whose first, second and third order partial derivatives, taken at
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
, are of respective order
o(n^{-1/3})
,
o(n^{-1/2})
and
o(n^{-1})
. These conditions assure that the value of
g_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is not oversensitive to the value of a single observation, and guarantee that the difference
g_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})-g_{n}(0,X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
is approximable by
X_{1}\times h_{n}(0,X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
for a function
h_{n}
; this latter condition controls the degree of non-linearity of
g_{n}
(see
Section

3
for a formal exposition). Exploiting these assumptions, we exactly characterize the limiting distribution of the bootstrap estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\rm boot}
, compare it to the distribution of the original statistic
\hat{\theta}_{n}
and study how fast the distribution of
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{{\rm boot}}
converges. This allows us to derive tight conditions on the functions
(g_{n})
and the process
(X_{i})
guaranteeing the consistency of the bootstrap method, and to study how the shape of the confidence intervals evolve when those conditions do not hold. Notably, we discover that when the mean of the observations
X_{1}
is unknown the bootstrap method is in general not consistent.
Moreover, we propose three alternative ways of using the bootstrap method to draw conservative confidence intervals with guaranteed minimum coverage. We illustrate our results by providing a series of simple examples, as well as examples derived from machine learning and econometrics, including: the p-value of kernel two-sample tests, the empirical risk of smooth stacked estimators, the value of min-max objectives, and confidence bands.

1.1
Related litterature

The empirical bootstrap method was first introduced in a breakthrough paper by Efron
[
23
]
. Other bootstraps methods have since been proposed including the multiplier bootstrap
[
47
]
, the residual bootstrap
[
18
]
or the non-remplacement bootstrap method
[
39
]
. A vast literature studies the theoretical properties of those techniques with some of the main results synthesized in the following books
[
28
,
18
,
31
,
3
]
. Most relevant to us are studies of the asymptotics of the bootstrap method. The consistency of the bootstrap method for linear statistics, t-statistics, Von-Mises functionals and quantiles has been established in
[
5
,
38
,
40
]
and for U-statistics in
[
1
,
48
]
.
Those results, among others, have been extended to high-dimensional regression and M-estimation
[
6
,
37
,
10
,
2
,
21
]
, misspecified models
[
41
]
, solutions of estimating equations
[
11
]
and to robust estimators
[
13
]
.In contrast, other works established the poor performance of the bootstrap method for non smooth statistics
[
22
,
4
,
5
]
, or for non-sparse high-dimensional regressions
[
24
]
.

Several recent breakthrough papers studied the consistency of the bootstrap method, both empirical and wild, for the maximum of high-dimensional averages with the dimension taken to be growing exponentially fast with the sample size. Notably
[
15
,
16
]
established the consistency of the bootstrap and gaussian approximation method when respectively
\log(p_{n}n)^{7/8}=o(n^{1/8})
and
\log(p_{n}n)^{7/6}=o(n^{1/6})
hold. A series of work have strengthen those results:
[
17
,
20
,
33
]
established the consistency of the multiplier and empirical bootstrap when
\log(pn)^{5/4}=o(n^{1/4})
,
[
35
]
established a quasi
\sqrt{n}^{-1}
rate for the wild bootstrap,
[
19
]
built slightly conservative confidence sets with guaranteed coverage under the conditions than
\log(p)=o({n})
and
[
14
]
proved that similar results hold for high-dimension U-statistics.
Those works use a combination of the Stein method, Edgeworth expansions, Lindeberg’s method
[
12
]
and the Slepian smart interpolation path. We note that the limiting distribution of those statistics are in general not Gaussian
[
20
]
.
Other works have studied the accuracy of the bootstrap method for specific statistics whose distributions are known to be asymptotically not Gaussian such as: the operator norm in high dimensions
[
36
,
29
,
32
]
, sampled eigenvalues of random matrices in high and moderate dimensions
[
25
]
or M-estimators having cube root convergence
[
9
]
.
The main contrast between this series of work and ours is that, rather than studying the bootstrap method for one specific statistics or application, we seek to establish the asymptotics of the bootstrap method under universal conditions on the estimators
(g_{n})
. Our proof builds on a breakthrough method proposed by Chatterjee
[
12
]
that generalized the Lindeberg method to a general technique to compare the expectations of
f(X_{1:n})
and
f(Y_{1:n})
of a large class of functions
f
.

2
Preliminaries

Let
\left(X^{n}_{i}\right)
be a triangular array of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d) processes with observations
X_{i}^{n}
taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
. Moreover, let
X^{n}=(X_{1}^{n},\ldots,X_{n}^{n})
denote its
n
-th row. Consider an estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}:=g_{n}(X^{n})
, where
g_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a measurable function, that we will typically refer to as a
statistic
, and let
(g_{n})
denote the sequence of measurable functions as
n
grows. To evaluate the performance of this estimator and build confidence intervals, we need to approximate its distribution. In this work, we will analyze the empirical bootstrap method.

Empirical bootstrap

Bootstrap samples
Z^{n}=(Z_{1}^{n},\ldots,Z_{n}^{n})
are sampled with replacement from the observations
\left\{X_{1}^{n},\dots,X_{n}^{n}\right\}
. This implies that conditionally on
X^{n}
the coordinates of
Z^{n}
are distributed i.i.d, with
Z_{i}^{n}\mid X^{n}\sim{\rm unif}\left(\left\{X_{1}^{n},\dots,X^{n}_{n}\right\}\right)
, for all
i\in[n]
.

Consistency metric and bootstrap consistency

Throughout the paper we denote with
Y^{n}=\left(Y_{1}^{n},\ldots,Y_{n}^{n}\right)
an independent copy of
X^{n}
. The bootstrap method is said to be consistent for
(g_{n})
if conditionally on
X^{n}
the distribution of
g_{n}(Z^{n})
well-approximates the distribution of
g_{n}(Y^{n})
, as
n\to\infty
. To make this statement rigorous we introduce a metric on the space of probability distributions. First, we define the class of three times continuously differentiable measurable functions with bounded third-order derivatives:

{\cal F}:=\left\{h\in C^{3}(\mathbb{R})\mid~\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|h^{(i)}(x)\right|\leq 1,~~\forall~1\leq i\leq 3\right\};

(2)

Given this, we define the distance on the space of probability measures, as the maximum mean discrepancy, where test functions range over the class
{\cal F}
:

d_{{\cal F}}(\mu,\nu):=\sup_{h\in{\cal F}}{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu,Y\sim\nu}\left[h(X)-h(Y)\right]}.

(3)

Moreover, we use the shorthand notation:

d_{{\cal F}}\left(\mu,\nu\mid{\cal E}\right):=\sup_{h\in{\cal F}}{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu,Y\sim\nu}\left[h(X)-h(Y)\mid{\cal E}\right]}.

(4)

This metric is related to the classical Levy-Prokhorov distance on probability spaces
[
7
]
. We say that
the empirical bootstrap method is consistent for
(g_{n})
if:

d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(Z^{n}),g_{n}(Y^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\xrightarrow{p}{0}.

(5)

Centering discrepancy and centered bootstrap consistency

Notably, an individual bootstrap sample
Z_{1}^{n}\mid X^{n}
, has a slightly different mean
\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{1}^{n}\mid X^{n}\right]=\bar{X}^{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}X_{i}^{n}
, than the one of
X_{1}^{n}
. As we will see this small difference plays a crucial role in determining the consistency of the bootstrap and for this reason it will be useful to define artificially centered versions of the random variables
(Z_{i}^{n})
and
(Y_{i}^{n})
. A centered bootstrap sample

\tilde{Z}^{n}_{i}:=Z^{n}_{i}-\left(\bar{X}^{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right]\right)

(6)

is a bootstrap sample that has been re-centered to artificially have the same mean than
X_{1}^{n}
. Moreover, denote with
\tilde{Y}_{i}^{n}
a corrected version of
Y_{i}^{n}
, artificially re-centered to have the same mean as
Z_{1}^{n}
, i.e.:

\tilde{Y}_{i}^{n}:=Y_{i}^{n}+\bar{X}^{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right].

(7)

Similarly we write
\tilde{Z}_{i}^{n}
a corrected version of
Z_{i}^{n}
, artificially re-centered to have the same mean as
X_{1}^{n}
, i.e.:

\tilde{Z}_{i}^{n}:=Z_{i}^{n}-\bar{X}^{n}+\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right].

(8)

We say that
the centered bootstrap is consistent for
(g_{n})
if:

d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n}),g_{n}(Y^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\xrightarrow{p}{0}.

(9)

From metric consistency to confidence intervals with nominal coverage

We can compare the confidence intervals of two random variables
X
and
Y
in terms of their mutual distance
d_{{\cal F}}(X,Y)
(proof in
Section

D.1
).

Proposition 1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two real-valued random variables and
{\cal E}
any random event. Let
\epsilon>0
be a constant then for any Borel set
A\in{\cal B}(\mathbb{R})
the following holds:

P(X\in A_{6\epsilon}\mid{\cal E})\geq P(Y\in A\mid{\cal E})-\frac{d_{{\cal F}}(X,Y\mid{\cal E})}{\epsilon^{3}},

(10)

where we wrote
A_{\epsilon}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid\exists y\in A~{\rm s.t}~|x-y|\leq\epsilon\}
. Moreover, if
[a,b]
is a confidence interval at level
1-\alpha
for
Y-\mathbb{E}\left[Y\mid{\cal E}\right]
, conditional on
{\cal E}
, then we have:

P\left(X-\mathbb{E}\left[X\mid{\cal E}\right]\in[a-6\epsilon,b+6\epsilon]\mid{\cal E}\right)\geq 1-\alpha-\frac{2d_{{\cal F}}(X,Y\mid{\cal E})}{\epsilon^{3}}.

(11)

For instance, suppose that we care about estimating
\theta_{n}:=\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n})\right]
. Then the bootstrap method, if consistent, can be used to build consistent confidence intervals for
\theta_{n}
. Indeed since we can estimate the conditional distribution of
\theta_{n}^{{\rm bootstrap}}:=g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})
, by drawing sufficiently many bootstrap sub-samples, we can find
C^{\alpha,n}
such that

P\left(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{{\rm bootstrap}}-\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\theta}_{n}^{{\rm bootstrap}}\mid X^{n}\right]\in C^{\alpha,n}\mid X^{n}\right)=1-\alpha.

(12)

Then, if we write
\hat{\theta}_{n}:=g_{n}(Y^{n})
, using the consistency of the bootstrap method we obtain that:

{\liminf_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}}\liminf_{n\to\infty}P\left(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}\in{C^{\alpha,n}_{\epsilon}}\right)\geq 1-\alpha.

(13)

Therefore, confidence intervals built using the bootstrap method achieve asymptotically nominal level of confidence. We note that prior works (e.g.
[
15
,
16
]
), typically provide a slightly stronger statement that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}P(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}\in C^{\alpha,n})\geq 1-\alpha
, by proving anti-concentration results on the limit distribution of
\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}
. Such anti-concentration, allows one to argue that the mass of the random variable
\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}
contained in
C_{\epsilon}^{\alpha,n}
converges to the mass contained in
C^{\alpha,n}
as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
and thereby,
\liminf_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\liminf_{n\to\infty}P(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}\in C_{\epsilon}^{\alpha,n})=\liminf_{n\to\infty}P(\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}\in C^{\alpha,n})
. Given that these results typically require stronger conditions on the statistic and many times Gaussian limits, we omit this step in this work and note that a slightly weaker, albeit still practically useful, statement on coverage is achievable in a more general setup.

2.1
Notations and definitions

For a scalar random variable
X
we denote with
\|X\|_{L_{p}}
, the
L_{p}
-norm:
\|X\|_{L_{p}}:=\mathbb{E}[X^{p}]^{1/p}
. Moreover, for vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote with
\|x\|_{p}
, the
\ell_{p}
vector norm:
\|x\|_{p}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{p}\right)^{1/p}
. For simplicity, given a sequence
(x_{i})
, with
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we shorthand

\displaystyle x_{1:n}~:=~

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),

\displaystyle x_{1:n}+c~:=~

\displaystyle(x_{1}+c,\dots,x_{n}+c),

\displaystyle cx_{2:n}~:=~

\displaystyle(c,x_{2},\dots,x_{n}).

(14)

We denote the
k
-th coordinate of
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
x_{i,k}
. For a function
f:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and a random variable
X
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
, we designate
f(\cdot+X)
the random function:
x_{1:n}\rightarrow f(x_{1:n}+X)
.

Lindenberg path interpolation

Let
Z^{n,i}
and
Z^{n,i,x}
be the following interpolating processes between
Z^{n}
and
\tilde{Y}^{n}
:

\displaystyle Z^{n,i}:=~

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{Y}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\tilde{Y}_{i}^{n},Z_{i+1}^{n},\ldots,Z_{n}^{n}\right)

(15)

\displaystyle Z^{n,i,x}:=~

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{Y}_{1}^{n},\ldots,\tilde{Y}_{i-1}^{n},x,Z_{i+1}^{n},\ldots,Z_{n}^{n}\right)

(16)

Higher-order derivatives and bounds

If a function
f
is three-times differentiable then we let:

\displaystyle\partial_{i,k}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i,k}}f(x_{1:n})

(17)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{i,k_{1:2}}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i,k_{1}}}\partial_{x_{i,k_{2}}}f(x_{1:n})

(18)

\displaystyle\partial^{3}_{i,k_{1:3}}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i,k_{1}}}\partial_{x_{i,k_{2}}}\partial_{x_{i,k_{3}}}f(x_{1:n})

(19)

Moreover, for a potentially random function
f
we define the constants:

\displaystyle M^{n}_{k}:=~

\displaystyle 2\,\|X_{1,k}^{n}\|_{L_{12}},

(20)

\displaystyle D_{k_{1}}^{n}(f)~:=~

\displaystyle M_{k_{1}}^{n}\,\max_{i\leq n}\left\|\partial_{i,k_{1}}f(Z^{n,i,\bar{X}^{n}})\right\|_{L_{12}}

(21)

\displaystyle D_{k_{1:2}}^{n}(f)~:=~

\displaystyle M_{k_{1}}^{n}\,M_{k_{2}}^{n}\,\max_{i\leq n}\left\|\partial^{2}_{i,k_{1:2}}f(Z^{n,i,\bar{X}^{n}})\right\|_{L_{12}}

(22)

\displaystyle D_{k_{1:3}}^{n}(f)~:=~

\displaystyle M_{k_{1}}^{n}\,M_{k_{2}}^{n}\,M_{k_{3}}^{n}\,\max_{i\leq n}\left\|\max_{x\in\left[\bar{X}^{n},\tilde{Y}^{n}_{1}\right]\cup\left[\bar{X}^{n},Z_{1}^{n}\right]}\partial^{3}_{i,k_{1:3}}f(Z^{n,i,x})\right\|_{L_{12}}

(23)

where for any two vectors
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote with
[a,b]
their convex closure, i.e.

[a,b]:=\{t\,a+(1-t)\,b:t\in[0,1]\}

(24)

3
Main Results

If the statistics
(g_{n})
were linear, i.e.
g_{n}(x_{1:n})=\sum_{i\leq n}x_{i}
, then the influence of a single observation
X_{1}^{n}
, on the estimate
\hat{\theta}_{n}
, would depend uniquely on the value of the random variable itself, i.e.
g_{n}(X^{n})-g(0X_{2:n}^{n})=X_{1}^{n}
. This is not the case for non-linear statistics. For instance, if
g_{n}(x_{1:n})=\max\left(\sum_{i\leq n}x_{i,1},\sum_{i\leq n}x_{i,2}\right)
, then the influence of observation
x_{1}
depends on the relative size of
\sum_{i>2}x_{i,1}
and
\sum_{i>2}x_{i,2}
.
In this paper, we want to study the asymptotics of the bootstrap method for such non-linear statistics, with complex influence functions. To control the degree of non-linearity, we assume that the statistics
(g_{n})
can be approximated by three times differentiable functions.

Assumption 1

(Approximability by
\mathbb{C}^{3}
)
.

There exists a sequence of functions
(f_{n})
with
f_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
s.t.:

1.

The functions
(f_{n})
approximate the estimators
(g_{n})
:

\displaystyle\left\|f_{n}(Z^{n})-g_{n}(Z^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}+\left\|f_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})-g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}\xrightarrow{n\to\,\infty}0.

(
H_{0}
)

2.

The first, second and third order derivatives are respectively of size
o(n^{-1/3})
,
o(n^{-1/2})
,
o(n^{-1})
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R_{n,1}~:=~&n^{1/3}\sum_{k_{1}\leq d_{n}}D_{k_{1}}^{n}(f_{n})=o(1)&R_{n,2}~:=~&\sqrt{n}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{n}}D_{k_{1:2}}^{n}(f_{n})=o(1)\\
R_{n,3}~:=~&n\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\leq d_{n}}D_{k_{1:3}}^{n}(f_{n})=o(1).\end{aligned}

(
H_{1}
)

To motivate
1
, we present two illustrating examples of simple estimators which fail to satisfy conditions (

H
0

), (

H
1

) and for which the bootstrap method is not consistent. Firstly we note that if (

H
0

) and (

H
1

) hold we have
\left\|g_{n}\left(X^{n}\right)-g_{n}\left(0X_{2:n}^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{3}}=o(n^{-1/3}).
This is a first-order stability property, i.e. that each sample
i
’s influence on the estimate has to decay at rate
n^{-1/3}
. Our first example is chosen to violate this.

Example 3.1
.

Let
(X_{i}^{n})
be a sequence of i.i.d observations distributed as
X_{i}\sim{\rm unif}(0,1)
. Let
(g_{n})
be the following sequence of functions:
g_{n}(x_{1:n}):=n\min_{i\leq n}x_{i}
. Then neither the bootstrap method nor the centered bootstrap method are consistent.
Moreover, we note that:
\left\|g_{n}(X_{1:n})-g_{n}(0X_{2:n})\right\|_{L_{3}}\propto n^{-1/3}
. In this example, the bootstrap estimator
g_{n}(Z^{n})\geq g_{n}(X)
is systematically larger than the original statistic, which leads to inconsistency of the bootstrap distribution.

Another consequence of having the second and third order derivative of respective order
o(n^{-1/2})
and
o(n^{-1})
is that the following two conditional expectations are very similar:

\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Z^{n})\mid X^{n}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\mid X^{n}\right]\right\|_{L_{1}}=o(1).

(25)

Our second example is chosen to satisfy the main implication of the first order stability conditions: i.e.
\|g_{n}(X^{n})-g_{n}(0X_{2:n}^{n})\|_{L_{3}}=o(n^{1/3})
; but to fail to respect this new property.

Example 3.2
.

Let
(X_{i}^{n})
be a sequence of i.i.d observations distributed as
X_{i}^{n}\sim{\rm unif}(0,1)
. Let
(g_{n})
be the following sequence of functions:

g_{n}(x_{1:n}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}1\left\{\min_{i\neq j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|>1/n\right\}-P\left(\min_{i\neq 1}\left|X_{1}-X_{i}\right|>1/n\right).

(26)

Then neither the bootstrap nor the centered bootstrap are consistent.
Moreover we note that the first order stability result holds, but
\left\|g_{n}(Y_{1:n})-g_{n}(0Y_{2:n})\right\|_{L_{3}}\propto n^{-1/2}
and Condition (
25
) is violated. The main driving force of inconsistency in this example is that contrary to the original sample, it is likely that the bootstrap sample will contain repeats. Hence we expect
P\left(\min_{i\neq 1}|Z_{i}^{n}-Z_{j}^{n}|>1/n\mid X^{n}\right)
to be smaller than
P\left(\min_{i\neq 1}|X_{1}-X_{i}|>1/n\right)
. See
Section

E.1
for formal proof.

Under
1
we study the limiting distribution of the bootstrap statistic and establish that it is asymptotically the same as
g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})
(proof in
Appendix

C
).

Theorem 1
.

Let
(g_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\to\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of measurable functions. Let
(X^{n}_{i})
be a triangular array of i.i.d processes such that
X^{n}_{1}\in L_{12}
. Under
1
, there is a constant
K
independent of
n
such that:

\begin{split}\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(Z^{n}),\,g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\leq~&\left\{\begin{split}&\left\|g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})-f_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}+\left\|g_{n}(Z^{n})-f_{n}(Z^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}\\
&~+K\,\left(R_{n,1}^{2}\max\left\{\frac{1}{n^{1/6}},R_{n,1}\right\}+R_{n,3}+R_{n,2}\right)\end{split}\right\}\to 0.\end{split}

Remark 1
.

We remark that the theorem also holds under slightly modified stability conditions. See
Theorem

13
in the appendix for more details.
Moreover, the hypothesis that
(X_{i}^{n})
is an i.i.d process can also be relaxed to assuming that the process
(X_{i}^{n})
is exchangeable. See
Theorem

14
for more details in the appendix.
Finally, note that
Theorem

1
can also be extended to random estimators
(g_{n})
, such as ones obtained by stochastic optimization methods (e.g SGD). See
Theorem

14
for more details in the appendix.

Theorem

1
guarantees that we can use the bootstrap method to estimate the distribution of
g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})
, which implies that it can also be used to build confidence intervals for
\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\mid X^{n}\right]
.

Corollary 1
.

Let
(g_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of measurable symmetric functions. Let
(X^{n}_{i})
be a triangular array of i.i.d processes such that
X^{n}_{1}\in L_{12}
. Assume that
(g_{n})
and
(X^{n}_{i})
satisfy all the conditions of
Theorem

1
. Then there is a constant
K
independent of
n
such that:

\begin{split}&\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(Z^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Z^{n})\mid X^{n}\right],\,g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\mid X^{n}\right]\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\\
&\leq 2\left\{\begin{split}&\left\|g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})-f_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}+\left\|g_{n}(Z^{n})-f_{n}(Z^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}\\
&~+K\,\left(R_{n,1}^{2}\max\left\{\frac{1}{n^{1/6}},R_{n,1}\right\}+R_{n,3}+R_{n,2}\right)\end{split}\right\}\to 0.\end{split}

However the distribution we are interested in is that of
g_{n}(Y^{n})
rather than
g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})
. Moreover, the shape of the confidence intervals of
g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})
can be arbitrary compared to the ones of
g_{n}(Y^{n})
, i.e. they are not systematically larger or smaller. This is illustrated in
Section

4
by a series of examples.
In
Section

3.1
we propose conditions that guarantee that the two distributions are asymptotically identical. In
Section

3.2
we prove that those conditions are tight and propose the use of the bootstrap method to build adjusted confidence intervals that are guaranteed to have at least (but not necessarily equal to) some minimum asymptotic coverage.

3.1
Stable Estimators to Uniform Perturbations

In this subsection we explore conditions guaranteeing that the distribution of
g_{n}(Y^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n})\right]
is asymptotically the same as the distribution of
g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\mid X^{n}\right]
, conditional on
X^{n}
, as this would imply that the bootstrap method provides consistent confidence intervals for
\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n})\right]
. We start by noting that if
(g_{n})
are linear then it automatically holds as we have

\sum_{i\leq n}\tilde{Y}_{i}^{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\leq n}\tilde{Y}_{i}^{n}\mid X^{n}\right]=\sum_{i\leq n}Y_{i}^{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\leq n}Y_{i}^{n}\mid X^{n}\right].

Observe that the random variables
\tilde{Y}_{i}^{n}
differ from
Y_{i}^{n}
in a benign manner: a random offset
\bar{X}^{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right]
, which is independent of
Y^{n}
, is added to all the random variables. Moreover, this offset is with high probability
O(n^{-1/2})
, since it is the difference of a sample and a population mean. We will refer to such perturbations of a sample
Y^{n}
as a
uniform perturbation
. To study general statistics, we introduce the following assumption which guarantees that small uniform perturbations do not drastically change the distribution of
g_{n}(Y^{n})
:

Assumption 2

(Stability to Uniform Perturbation)
.

A statistic sequence
(g_{n})
is stable to small uniform perturbations if for all
B>0
:

\displaystyle r^{n,B}:=~\left\|\sup_{x\in B_{d_{n}}(0,B)}\left|g_{n}(X^{n}+x/{\sqrt{n}})-g_{n}(X^{n})-\mathbb{E}\Big[g_{n}(X^{n}+x/{\sqrt{n}})-g_{n}(X^{n})\Big]\right|\right\|_{L_{1}}\overset{n\to\infty}{\to}0

(
H_{2}
)

where we define
B_{d_{n}}(0,B):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\mid\|x\|_{2}\leq B\}
.

Note that the perturbations considered in hypothesis (

H
2

) are uniform on all the coordinates
i\in[n]
. This notably implies that if
g_{n}
depends only the relative distance between the observations then hypothesis (

H
2

) holds. We prove, under hypothesis (

H
2

), that the bootstrap method is consistent and hence by
1
can be used to build asymptotically consistent confidence intervals for
\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(X^{n})\right]
(proof in
Section

E.2
).

Theorem 2
.

Let
(g_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of measurable functions. Let
(X^{n}_{i})
be a triangular array of i.i.d processes such that
X^{n}_{1}\in L_{12}
. Assume that
(g_{n})
satisfies
1
and
2
.
Then there exists a universal constant
K
such that:

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(Z^{n})-\mathbb{E}\big[g_{n}(Z^{n})|X^{n}],\,g_{n}(Y^{n})-\mathbb{E}(g_{n}(Y^{n}))\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\\
\leq\inf_{B_{n}\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\begin{gathered}\left\|g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})-f_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}+\big\|g_{n}(Z^{n})-f_{n}(Z^{n})\big\|_{L_{1}}+K(R_{n,1})^{2}\max\left\{\frac{1}{n^{1/6}},R_{n,1}\right\}\\
+K\left(R_{n,3}+R_{n,2}\right)+\frac{2\sqrt{\sum_{k\leq k_{n}}\|X_{1,k}^{n}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}}}{B_{n}}\Big[\big\|g_{n}(Y^{n})\big\|_{L_{2}}+\big\|g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\big|_{L_{2}}\Big]+r^{n,B_{n}}\end{gathered}\right\}\to 0.

(27)

Condition (

H
2

) holds beyond linear statistics. We present two simple illustrative examples of such non-linear estimators, for which (

H
2

) is satisfied.

Example 3.3
.

Let
(X_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence of random variables taking value in
\mathbb{R}
. We suppose that they are bounded:
\|X_{1}\|_{L_{\infty}}<\infty
. We define the functions
(g_{n,1})
and
(g_{n,2})
as satisfying:

g_{n,1}:x_{1:n}\rightarrow\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor}x_{i}-x_{i+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\right)^{2},\qquad g_{n,2}:x_{1:n}\rightarrow\sqrt{n}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\frac{x_{i}-\bar{x}^{n}}{n}\right)-1\Big].

Then the functions
(g_{n,1},g_{n,2})
satisfy conditions (

H
0

), (

H
1

) and (

H
2

). Hence the bootstrap is consistent, i.e.:

d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n,1}(Z^{n})-\mathbb{E}(g_{n,1}(Z^{n})|X),g_{n,1}(Y_{1:n})-\mathbb{E}(g_{n,1}(Y_{1:n}))\mid X\right)\rightarrow 0;

d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n,2}(Z^{n})-\mathbb{E}(g_{n,2}(Z^{n})|X),g_{n,2}(Y_{1:n})-\mathbb{E}(g_{n,2}(Y_{1:n}))\mid X\right)\rightarrow 0.

However hypothesis (

H
2

) can be easily violated by simple examples. We prove in the next subsection, under mild conditions, that
violation of (

H
2

) implies that no re-sampling method can provide asymptotically consistent confidence intervals
. We present here a simple example of this phenomenon.

Example 3.4
.

Let
(X_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence of scalar-valued, bounded observations with mean
0
. Write
(Y_{i})
an independent copy of
(X_{i})
. Define the following functions
g_{n}:x_{1:n}\rightarrow\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}x_{i}\right)^{2}
. Then hypothesis (

H
2

) does not hold and the centered distributions of
(g_{n}(Y_{1:n}))
and
(g_{n}(\tilde{Y}_{1:n}))
are not asymptotically identical

d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(\tilde{Y}_{1:n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(\tilde{Y}_{1:n})\mid X\right],\,g_{n}(Y_{1:n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y_{1:n})\right]\mid X\right)\not\rightarrow 0.

(28)

3.2
Impossibility for Unstable Estimators to Uniform Perturbations

In this section we prove that if the estimators are sensitive to small uniform perturbations then the bootstrap method is not consistent. Then we offer three solutions on how to use the bootstrap to build confidence intervals with a guaranteed minimum coverage.

Non-consistency of the bootstrap if the estimators are unstable.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{n}
be a class of probability distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
.
Write
\mathcal{P}_{M}(\mathbb{R})
the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
. We say that the centered distribution of
g_{n}(\cdot)
can be estimated over the class of distributions
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{n}
if there is a measurable function
\mathcal{Q}_{n}:x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{M}(\mathbb{R})
such that for all sequences of distributions
(\nu_{n})\in\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{n}
we have

\mathbb{E}_{X^{n}\sim\nu_{n}}\left[\left|d_{{\cal F}}\left(\mathcal{Q}_{n}(X^{n}),\,g_{n}(Y^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n})\right]\mid X^{n}\right)\right|\right]\xrightarrow{n\rightarrow\infty}0;

(29)

where
(Y_{i}^{n})
is taken to be to be an independent copy of
(X_{i}^{n})
.
We prove that the centered distribution of
g_{n}(\cdot)
cannot be estimated if a hypothesis similar to (

H
2

) is not respected (proof in
Section

E.4
).

Theorem 3
.

Let
(g_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of measurable functions. Define
\Omega_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
to be a non empty open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
and let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{n}:=\{p_{\theta}^{n},\theta\in\Omega_{n}\}
be a parametric subset of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim p^{n}_{\theta}}(X)=\theta
. Denote
(\mathcal{I}_{n}(\theta))
the Fisher information matrix of
(p^{n}_{\theta})
. Suppose that there is a sequence of measures
(p^{n}_{\theta_{n}})\in\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{n}
, a sequence
(z_{n})\in\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
and a real
\epsilon>0
such that

(i).

\limsup\sup_{\tilde{\theta}_{n}\in\left[\theta_{n},\,\theta_{n}+\frac{z_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right]}\left\|\mathcal{I}_{n}(\tilde{\theta}_{n})^{1/2}\frac{z_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\|_{2}<\infty
.

(ii).

The following holds if
(X_{i}^{n})\overset{i.i.d}{\sim}p^{n}_{\theta_{n}}

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}\left(X^{n}+\frac{z_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}\left(X^{n}+\frac{z_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right],\,g_{n}(X^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(X^{n})\right]\right)>\epsilon.

(30)

(iii).

\theta_{n}+\frac{z_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\in\Omega_{n}

Then for all measurable functions
\mathcal{Q}_{n}:x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{M}(\mathbb{R})
there is a sequence
(\nu_{n})\in\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{n}
such that:

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(\mathcal{Q}_{n}(X^{n}),g_{n}(Y^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n})\right]\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\not\rightarrow{}0

(31)

where
(X_{i}^{n}),(Y_{i}^{n})\overset{i.i.d}{\sim}\nu_{n}.

Theorem

3
implies that if the means of the observations are unknown then no re-sampling method will in general be consistent. We propose in
Section

3.2.1
,
Section

3.2.2
and
Section

3.2.3
three alternative ways to build confidence intervals, that bypass this impossibility result and have asymptotically a guaranteed coverage of at least
1-\alpha
. Albeit, some of these intervals will potentially have larger size than needed.

3.2.1
Consistency of the Centered-Bootstrap

First we explore the case when we know the mean of the observations
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right]
. In this case, we can leverage this knowledge to build centered bootstrap samples
\tilde{Z}_{i}^{n}:=Z_{i}^{n}+\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right]-\bar{X}^{n}
. Observe that these centered samples satisfy the crucial property that

\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{Z}_{1}^{n}\mid X^{n}\right]=\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{n})
. We prove that, under mild conditions, the centered bootstrap estimator is asymptotically consistent. The conditions needed for the centered bootstrap to be consistent are hypothesis
(H_{0})
and
(H_{1})
formulated instead for
(Y^{n}_{i})
and
(\tilde{Z}_{i}^{n})
rather than for
(\tilde{Y}^{n}_{i})
and
(Z_{i}^{n})
.

Assumption 3

(Approximation by
\mathbb{C}^{3}
of
g_{n}(\cdot+\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{n})-\bar{X}^{n})
.)
.

There exists a sequence of functions
(f_{n})
with
f_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
s.t.:

1.

The functions
(f_{n})
approximate the estimators
(g_{n})
:

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\big\|f_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})-g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})\big\|_{L_{1}}+\big\|f_{n}(Y^{n})-g_{n}(Y^{n})\Big\|_{L_{1}}\xrightarrow{\beta\rightarrow\infty}0.

(
H^{{\rm c}}_{0}
)

2.

The first, second and third order derivatives are respectively of size
o(n^{-1/3})
,
o(n^{-1/2})
,
o(n^{-1})
:

\displaystyle R_{n,1}^{{\rm c}}:=n^{1/3}\sum_{k_{1}\leq d_{n}}D_{1,k_{1}}^{n}\left(f_{n}(\cdot+\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{n})-\bar{X}^{n})\right)=o(1);

(32)

\displaystyle R_{n,2}^{{\rm c}}:=\sqrt{n}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\leq d_{n}}D_{2,k_{1:2}}^{n}\left(f_{n}(\cdot+\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{n})-\bar{X}^{n})\right)=o(1);

(
H^{{\rm c}}_{1}
)

\displaystyle R_{n,3}^{{\rm c}}:=n\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\leq d_{n}}D_{3,k_{1:3}}^{n}\left(f_{n}(\cdot+\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{n})-\bar{X}^{n})\right))=o(1).

(33)

We show that under those conditions the centered bootstrap is asymptotically consistent and thereby can be used to build confidence intervals with asymptotically nominal coverage.

Theorem 4
.

Let
(g_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of measurable functions. Let
(X^{n}_{i})
be a triangular array of i.i.d processes such that
X^{n}_{1}\in L_{12}
. Assume that there is a sequence
(f_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R})
of measurable functions satisfying conditions
(H^{{\rm c}}_{0})
and
(H^{{\rm c}}_{1})
.
Then there is a universal constant
K
such that:

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n}),\,g_{n}(Y^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\leq\left\{\begin{gathered}\left\|g_{n}(Y^{n})-f_{n}(Y^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}+\left\|g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})-f_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})\right\|_{L_{1}}\\
+K\left((R_{n,1}^{c})^{2}\max\left\{\frac{1}{n^{1/6}},R_{n,1}^{c}\right\}+R_{n,3}^{c}+R_{n,2}^{c}\right)\end{gathered}\right\}\to 0.

(34)

Example 3.5

(Application to hypothesis testing)
.

An important application is hypothesis testing. Suppose we want to test
(H_{0}):~\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right]=\theta
against an alternative
(H_{1})
. In this goal, we build a test statistic
\hat{T}_{n}(X^{n})
for which we want to compute a p-value. Let
(Z_{i}^{n})
be a bootstrap sample of
\{X_{1}^{n},\dots,X_{n}^{n}\}
; define
(Z^{\theta}_{i})
as the following process:

Z^{\theta}_{i}:=Z^{n}_{i}-\bar{X}^{n}+\theta.

(35)

We remark that under the null,
(Z^{\theta}_{i})
is a centered bootstrap sample of
X^{n}
. Using
Theorem

4
we know, under stability conditions on
(\hat{T}_{n})
(i.e.
3
), that we can use
\hat{T}^{n}(Z^{\theta}_{1:n})
to estimate the p-value of
\hat{T}_{n}
.

Proposition 2
.

Let
(X_{i}^{n})
be a triangular array of i.i.d processes taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
and
\hat{T}_{n}:\times_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of measurable functions that satisfies
3
. Then:

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(\hat{T}_{n}(Z^{\theta}_{1:n}),\hat{T}_{n}(Y_{1:n}^{n,\theta})\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\to 0.

3.2.2
Corrected Confidence Interval

We now investigate two distinct methods to build conservative confidence intervals, when the mean
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{n}]
is not known.

According to
1
the bootstrap method can be used to build consistent confidence intervals for
\mathbb{E}(g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\mid X^{n})
. Therefore if we can bound the distance from
\mathbb{E}(g_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})\mid X^{n})
to
\mathbb{E}(g_{n}(Y^{n}))
we can use the bootstrap method to build confidence intervals on the latter. This is the first method that we propose. To do so we exploit the fact that under mild conditions
\sqrt{n}\big[\bar{X^{n}}-\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{n})\big]
is approximately normal. We assume that the function
x\rightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n}+x)\right]
is
\alpha
-Holder and that the moments of
X_{1}^{n}
are bounded. More formally, suppose that there is a sequence
(C_{n})
and a constant
b
such that:

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n}+\frac{x}{\sqrt{n}})-g_{n}(Y^{n})\right]\Big|\leq C_{n}\max_{k\leq d_{n}}|x_{k}|^{\alpha},\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}

(
H_{3}
)

\displaystyle\min_{j\leq d_{n}}\|X^{n}_{1,j}\|_{L_{3}}\geq b,\quad\frac{\log(d_{n})^{7/6}\|\sup_{k\leq d_{n}}|X^{n}_{1,k}|\|_{L_{4}}^{4}}{n^{1/6}}=o(1).

(36)

Theorem 5
.

Let
(g_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of measurable functions satisfying Assumption 1 and (

H
3

). Denote
\Sigma_{n}
the variance-covariance matrix of
X_{1}^{n}
and
(N^{n})
to be a sequence of Gaussian vectors distributed as
N^{n}\sim N(0,\Sigma_{n})
.
Let
\beta>0
be a real; write
t_{g,n}^{\beta/2}
and
t_{{\rm b},n}^{\beta/2}(X^{n})
as quantities satisfying

P\left(\big|g_{n}(Z^{n})-\mathbb{E}(g_{n}(Z^{n})|X^{n})\big|\geq t_{{\rm b},n}^{\beta/2}(X^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\leq\beta/2;

P\left(\max_{k}\big|N^{n}_{k}|\geq(t_{g,n}^{\beta/2})^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}{C_{n}}^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)\leq\beta/2.

Then the following holds:

\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n}P\left(\mathbb{E}(g_{n}(Y^{n}))\leq\big[g_{n}(Z^{n})-t_{{\rm b},n}^{\beta/2}-t_{g,n}^{\beta/2}-\delta,~~g_{n}(Z^{n})+t_{{\rm b},n}^{\beta/2}+t_{g,n}^{\beta/2}+\delta\big]\right)\leq\beta.

See
Section

E.6
for proof of
Theorem

5
. We present here an illustrative example, and in
Section

6
present an application of
Theorem

5
to a classical problem.

Example 3.6
.

Let
(X_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence with mean
0
and variance
1
. Suppose that
X_{i}\in L_{12}
and let
c_{\alpha}(X^{n})
be such that:

P\left(\left|\big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}Z^{n}_{i}\big]^{2}-\mathbb{E}\big(\big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}Z^{n}_{i}\mid X\big]^{2}\big)\right|\geq c_{\alpha}(X)\big|X\right)\leq\alpha.

Denote
z_{\alpha}
the
1-\alpha
quantile of a standard normal:
P\left(Z\geq z_{\alpha}\right)\leq\alpha
where
Z\geq N(0,1)
. Then the following holds:

\limsup_{n\rightarrow 0}P\left(\left|\big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}Z^{n}_{i}\big]^{2}-\mathbb{E}\big(\big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}X_{i}\big]^{2}\big)\right|\geq c_{\alpha/2}(X)+z_{\alpha/4}^{2}\right)\leq\alpha

The second method exploits the bootstrap method for slightly shifted observations. The goal is to use the fact that under moderate conditions we know that
\left\|\bar{X^{n}}-\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{n})\right\|
is of size
O(1/\sqrt{n})
. In this goal, we denote
B_{d_{n}}(\gamma)
the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
of radius
\gamma
for the Euclidean-norm.

Theorem 6
.

Let
(g_{n})
be a sequence of measurable functions. Suppose that for all sequence
(\mu_{n})\in B_{d_{n}}(\gamma_{n})

1
is satisfied by
(X^{n}+\mu_{n})
and
(g_{n})
. Define
(\gamma_{n})
to be a sequence such that
\frac{\sqrt{n}\gamma_{n}}{\log(d_{n})}\rightarrow\infty.

Set
t_{*}^{\alpha}(X^{n})
to be satisfying

\sup_{\mu\in B_{d_{n}}(\gamma_{n})}P\left(\left|g_{n}(Z^{n}+\mu)-\mathbb{E}\left(g_{n}(Z^{n}+\mu)\mid X^{n}\right)\right|\geq t_{*}^{\alpha}(X^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\leq\alpha.

Then the following holds:

\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n\rightarrow 0}P\left(\left|g_{n}(Y^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left(g_{n}(Y^{n})\right)\right|\geq t_{*}^{\alpha}(X^{n})+\delta\mid X^{n}\right)\leq\alpha.

See
Section

E.8
for a proof. We apply this new result to the previous illustrative example.

Example 3.7
.

Let
(X_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence with mean
0
and variance
1
. Suppose that
X_{i}\in L_{12}
and let
c_{\alpha}(X^{n})
be such that:

\sup_{x,|x|\leq\log(n)/\sqrt{n}}P\left(\left|\big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}Z_{i}+x\big]^{2}-\mathbb{E}\big(\big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}Z_{i}+x\big]^{2}\big)\right|\geq c_{\alpha}(X^{n})\big|X^{n}\right)\leq\alpha.

Then the following holds:

\limsup_{n\rightarrow 0}P\left(\left|\big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}Y_{i}\big]^{2}-\mathbb{E}\big(\big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}Y_{i}\big]^{2}\big)\right|\geq c_{\alpha}(X^{n})\right)\leq\alpha

3.2.3
Robust Confidence Interval

Theorem

4
states that if the mean
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right]
of the observations is known then we can instead study the centered bootstrap estimator, which under technical conditions, is asymptotically consistent. However assuming that the mean is known can be unrealistic. In this section, we instead assume that we know that it belongs to a certain subset
A_{n}
and seek to find a confidence interval with a guaranteed coverage level for all potential values of the mean. To make this more precise, we consider an adversary that can see the draw of the random samples and translate them by any offset in the translation set
B_{n}:=\{x-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{1}^{n}\right]:x\in A_{n}\}
. Our goal is to guarantee that no-matter what perturbation the adversary chooses, we produce a confidence interval with guaranteed coverage. Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
a set of probability distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
such that there exists a sequence of functions
(f_{n})
with
f_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
such that:

1.

The functions
(f_{n})
approximate the estimators
(g_{n})
:

\displaystyle\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\mathbb{E}_{X^{n}{\sim}\nu^{\otimes\infty}}\left(\left|f_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})-g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})\right|\right)+\mathbb{E}_{X^{n}{\sim}\nu^{\otimes\infty}}\left(\left|f_{n}({X}^{n})-g_{n}({X}^{n})\right|\right)\xrightarrow{n\to\,\infty}0.

(
H^{rob}_{0}
)

2.

The first, second and third order derivatives are such that:

\displaystyle\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\max\left(R_{n,1}^{c,\nu},R_{n,2}^{c,\nu},R_{n,3}^{c,\nu}\right)\rightarrow 0.

(
H_{1}^{rob}
)

where for each distribution
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we denoted by
R_{n,1}^{c,\nu}
,
R_{n,2}^{c,\nu}
and
R_{n,3}^{c,\nu}
the coefficients
R_{n,1}^{c}
,
R_{n,2}^{c}
and
R_{n,3}^{c}
computed for
(X^{n}_{i})\overset{i.i.d}{\sim}\nu
.

Our goal we is to use the bootstrap method to find
(t_{n}^{\alpha}(X^{n}))
such that the following holds:

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\nu}(X)\in A_{n}\end{subarray}}{{P_{X^{n},Y^{n}\overset{i.i.d}{\sim}\nu}\left(\left|g_{n}(Y^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n})\right]\right|\geq t_{n}^{\alpha}(X^{n})\right)}}\leq\alpha.

(37)

If conditions
(H_{0}^{{\rm rob}})
and
(H_{1}^{{\rm rob}})
hold then the bootstrap method can be used to find a sequence
(t_{n}^{\alpha})
such that
eq.

37
holds (proof in
Section

E.9
).

Theorem 7
.

Let
(g_{n})
be a sequence of measurable functions, let
(\mathcal{P}_{n})
be sets of probability measures chosen such that
(H_{0}^{\rm{rob}})
and
(H_{1}^{\rm{rob}})
hold. For all
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and given a sample
X^{n}\sim\nu
define
t_{n}^{\alpha}(X^{n})
to be such that:

\sup_{\mu\in A_{n}}{P\left(\left|g_{n}(Z^{n}+\mu-\bar{X^{n}})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Z^{n}+\mu-\bar{X^{n}})\mid X^{n}\right]\right|\geq t_{n}^{\alpha}(X^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)}\leq\alpha.

(38)

Then if we write
\mathcal{Q}_{n}:=\{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\mid\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\nu}(X)\in A_{n}\}
then the following holds

\liminf_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\nu\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}{{P_{X^{n},Y^{n}\overset{i.i.d}{\sim}\nu}\left(\left|g_{n}(Y^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left[g_{n}(Y^{n})\right]\right|\geq t_{n}^{\alpha}(X^{n})+\delta\right)}}\leq\alpha.

(39)

4
Illustrative Examples and Counterexamples

We present a sequence of simple examples illustrating that our theorems hold even if the estimator is not asymptotically normal. Moreover, we provide negative examples where the shape of the confidence intervals obtained by the bootstrap method can be arbitrary compared to the ones of the original statistics
g_{n}(Y_{1:n})
. The first example we consider are polynomials of the empirical average. Their limiting distribution is in not Gaussian for
p>1
.

Example 4.1
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
be an integer and let
X:=(X_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence taking value in
\mathbb{R}
with mean
0
and admitting a
12p
-th moment
\mathbb{E}\left[\big|X_{i}|^{12p}\right]<\infty
. We define the functions
(g_{n})
as
g_{n}:x_{1:n}\rightarrow\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}x_{i}\right)^{p}.
We write
(Z^{n}_{{i}})
a bootstrap sample and
(Y^{n}_{i})
an independent copy of
X
. Then the following holds:

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}\left(Z^{n}\right),\left(\sqrt{n}~\bar{X}^{n}+\sqrt{n}~\bar{Y}^{n}\right)^{p}\mid X\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

Moreover, let
A
be an
1-\alpha
confidence-interval for
g_{n}(\tilde{Y}_{1:n})
meaning
P(g_{n}(\tilde{Y}_{1:n})\in A)\geq 1-\alpha
. Write:
A_{\bar{X}^{n}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid~\exists y\in A~{\rm s.t}~x={\rm sign}(x)]\left(|y|^{1/p}-\bar{X}^{n}\right)^{p}\}
then

P(g_{n}(Y_{1:n})\in A_{\bar{X}^{n}})\geq 1-\alpha.

See
Section

F.1
for the proof.

Example 4.2
.

Let
X:=(X_{i})
be an i.i.d sequence of bounded real valued random variables satisfying
\mathbb{E}(X_{1})=0
. We define
g_{n}:\times_{l=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
to be the following function:
g_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=\sqrt{n}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\frac{x_{i}}{{n}}\right)-1\Big]
. Write
(\tilde{Z}_{i}^{n})
to be a centered bootstrap sample and let
Y:=(Y_{i})
be an independent copy of
X
. Then the following holds:

\Big\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})-\mathbb{E}(g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})|X),g_{n}(Y_{1:n})-\mathbb{E}(g_{n}(Y^{n}))\Big|X\right)\Big\|_{L_{1}}\rightarrow 0.

See
Section

F.2
for a formal proof.

The next example demonstrates that the confidence intervals obtained by the bootstrap method are neither systematically bigger or smaller than the ones of original statistics.

Example 4.3
.

Let
(X_{i})
be a sequence of i.i.d standard normal observations
X_{i}\sim N(0,1)
. Define
(g_{n})
to be the following sequence of functions:
g_{n}(x_{1:n}):=\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}x_{i}\right]^{+}
. Let
(Z^{n})
be a bootstrap sample. The following holds:

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(Z^{n}),\,\sqrt{n}\left[\bar{Y}+\bar{X}\right]^{+}\mid X\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\rightarrow 0.

(40)

Moreover given
\alpha<0.5
and a sequence
(t_{n})
such that:
P(g_{n}(Z^{n})\leq t_{n}\mid X^{n})=1-\alpha
then:

P(g_{n}(Y^{n})\leq t_{n}-\sqrt{n}\bar{X}^{n})\sim 1-\alpha.

(41)

We notice that the segment
[0,t]
is smaller than
[0,t-\sqrt{n}\bar{X}^{n}]
only if
\sqrt{n}\bar{X}^{n}>0
which asymptotically happens with a probability of
1/2
.

See
Section

F.3
for a formal proof.

In the next example we show that our results apply to classical quantities in mathematical physics. We consider the entropy of spin glasses configurations.

Example 4.4
.

Let
X:=(X_{i,j})
be an array of i.i.d observations satisfying
X_{i,j}\overset{i.i.d}{\sim}N(0,1)
. We denote
X^{n}:=\left(X_{i,j}\right)_{i,j\leq n}
the induced matrix and define
g_{n}:M_{n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
to be the following function:
g_{n}(X):=\frac{1}{n}\log\left(\sum_{m\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}e^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}m^{\top}X^{n}m}\right)
. Write
(Z_{i,j}^{n})
and
(\tilde{Z}_{i,j}^{n})
respectively a bootstrap and centered bootstrap sample and
Y^{n}
an independent copy of
X^{n}
. Then the following holds:

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(Z^{n}),\,\frac{1}{n}\log\left(\sum_{m\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}e^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}m^{\top}Y^{n}m}~e^{\bar{X}^{n}(\sum_{i}m_{i})^{2}/\sqrt{n}}\right)\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\rightarrow 0;

(42)

and

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(g_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n}),\,\frac{1}{n}\log\left(\sum_{m\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}e^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}m^{\top}Y^{n}m}\right)\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}\rightarrow 0;

(43)

where we have denoted
\bar{X}^{n}:=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\leq n}X_{i,j}^{n}
.

See
Section

F.4
for a formal proof.

5
Uniform Confidence Bands

In this section, we study the maximum of centered empirical processes. This is motivated by its application to uniform confidence bounds (see e.g.
[
15
,
16
]
). Let
(X_{i}^{n})
be a triangular array of i.i.d process with
X_{1}^{n}
taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{p_{n}}
where
(p_{n})
is an increasing sequence. We want to estimate the distribution of

\max_{j\leq p_{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}X_{i,j}^{n}-\mathbb{E}\big[X_{1,j}^{n}\big].

For fast growing sequences of
(p_{n})
this statistics is not asymptotically Gaussian
[
20
]
. Therefore to study its distribution one might want to use the bootstrap method. Using our results we recover the results of
[
20
]
and establish conditions under which the bootstrap is asymptotically consistent (proof in
Section

G.1
).

Proposition 3
.

Let
(p_{n})
be a sequence of integers satisfying
\log(p_{n})=o(n^{1/4})
. Define
(X^{n}_{i})
to be a triangular array of sequences of i.i.d random variables taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{p_{n}}
. We suppose that
\left\|\sup_{k\leq p_{n}}\left|X_{1,k}^{n}\right|\right\|_{L_{12}}<\infty
.
We denote
\mathcal{M}_{n}(x_{1:n})=\max_{j\leq p_{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}x_{i,j}
.
Then the following holds

\Big\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{n}(Z^{n}-\bar{X}^{n}),~\mathcal{M}_{n}(Y^{n}-\mathbb{E}[Y^{n}_{1}])\mid X^{n}\right)\Big\|_{L_{1}}=o(1).

6
Value of a Min-Max Objective

In this section, motivated by problems in sample average approximations of stochastic linear programs
[
27
]
and in structural econometric problems
[
42
]
, we want to estimate distribution of the value of a stochastic min-max objective. Given a set of potential actions
\{\theta_{i}\}
, a payoff function
f_{n}
and random states of nature
(\xi^{n}_{\ell})
we wish to estimate the expected payoff of a stochastic min-max objective:

\theta_{0}:=\min_{i\leq p_{n}}\max_{j\leq p_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{n}(\xi^{n}_{\ell},u_{i},v_{j})\right].

(44)

We assume we have access to
n
i.i.d. samples of
\xi^{n}:=(\xi_{1^{n}},\ldots,\xi_{n}^{n})
and want to build confidence intervals for
\theta_{0}
. To achieve this, we will estimate the distribution of the root-
n
normalized version of the empirical analogue of the stochastic program:

\min_{i\leq p_{n}}\max_{j\leq p_{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{\ell\leq n}f_{n}(\xi^{n}_{\ell},u_{i},v_{j}).

(45)

We will investigate the consistency of the bootstrap method for this problem. For ease of notations, we write
X_{\ell,i,j}^{n}:=f_{n}(\xi^{n}_{\ell},i,j)
, and set
(p_{n})
to be an increasing sequence and write:

\hat{\theta}_{n}(x_{1:n}):=\min_{i\leq p_{n}}\max_{j\leq p_{n}}~\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\ell\leq n}x_{\ell,i,j}.

(46)

We show that the centered bootstrap method is consistent as long as
\log(p_{n})=o(n^{1/4})
(proof in
Section

H.1
).

Proposition 4
.

Let
(p_{n})
be a sequence of integers satisfying
\log(p_{n})=o(n^{1/4})
. Define
(X^{n}_{i})
to be a triangular array of sequences of i.i.d random variables taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{p_{n}\times p_{n}}
. Suppose that
\|\sup_{l_{1},l_{2}\leq p_{n}}|X_{1,l_{1},l_{2}}^{n}|\|_{L_{12}}<\infty
.
Then the following holds

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(\sqrt{n}\Big[\hat{\theta}_{n}(Z^{n})-\theta_{Z^{n}}\Big],~\sqrt{n}\Big[\hat{\theta}_{n}(\tilde{Y}^{n})-\theta_{\tilde{Y}^{n}}\Big]\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}=o(1);

\left\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})-\theta_{\tilde{Z}^{n}}\right),~\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}_{n}(Y^{n})-\theta_{0}\right)\mid X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{1}}=o(1);

where for a process
(Y^{\prime}_{1:n})
we have written, by abuse of notations,

\theta_{Y^{\prime}}:=\min_{i\leq p_{n}}\max_{j\leq p_{n}}~\mathbb{E}\big[Y^{{}^{\prime}}_{l,i,j}|X^{n}\big].

Therefore if we let
t_{n,\alpha}
to be a threshold such that:

\Pr\left(\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{n})-\theta_{\tilde{Z}^{n}}\right)\geq t_{n,\alpha}\mid X^{n}\right)=\alpha

(47)

Then for every sequence
(\epsilon_{n})
satisfying
\epsilon_{n}\downarrow 0
and
\left(\frac{\log(p_{n})^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{1/9}=o(\epsilon_{n})
we have

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr\left(\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}(X^{n})-\theta_{0}\right)\geq t_{n,\alpha}+\epsilon_{n}\right)\leq\alpha

(48)

We note that if the means are sufficiently spaced:

\frac{\log(p_{n})}{\sqrt{n}}=o\Big[\inf_{(i,j)\neq(l,k)}\big|\mathbb{E}\big[X^{n}_{1,l,k}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[X^{n}_{1,i,j}\big]\big|\Big]

then the bootstrap method is consistent. See
Lemma

25
for a precise statement and a proof.

We generalize those results to estimating the payoff of minmax strategies over a continuous space. Let
(d^{\prime}_{n})
be a non-decreasing sequence and denote
B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}_{n}}
the ball of radius
1
in
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}_{n}}
. Choose
(f_{n}:\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\times B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}\times B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1})
to be a sequence of Lipschitz functions for which there are constants
(C_{n})
and
(c_{n})
satisfying:

\displaystyle\left|f_{n}(x,u,v)-f_{n}(x,u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\right|<~

\displaystyle C_{n}\left(\|u-u^{\prime}\|+\|v-v^{\prime}\|\right)

(
H^{{\rm minmax}}_{0}
)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f_{n}(X_{1}^{n},u,v)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[f_{n}(X_{1}^{n},u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\right]\right|>~

\displaystyle c_{n}\left(\|u-u^{\prime}\|+\|v-v^{\prime}\|\right)

(
H^{{\rm minmax}}_{1}
)

Similarly as in the discrete case our goal is to estimate

\theta_{n}:=\inf_{u\in B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}}\sup_{v\in B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{n}(\xi_{1}^{n},u,v)\right]

(49)

and to do so we propose as an estimator

\hat{\theta}_{n}(X^{n}):=\inf_{u\in B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}}\sup_{v\in B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}}\frac{1}{{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}f_{n}(\xi_{i}^{n},u,v).

(50)

To build confidence intervals around
\theta_{n}
, we want to use the bootstrap method. Using
Theorem

1
, we prove that it is consistent under distinct set of assumptions: 1) If
the dimensions
(d^{\prime}_{n})
grow as
o(n^{1/4})
and if
the sequences
(C_{n})
and
(c_{n})
are bounded respectively from bellow and above or 2) If
the dimensions
(d^{\prime}_{n})
grow as
o(n^{1/7})
and if
the sequences
(C_{n})
is bounded from above (proof in
Section

H.2
).

Proposition 5
.

Let
\left(f_{n}:\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\times B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}\times B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}\right)
be a sequence of Lipschitz functions
satisfying condition (

H
minmax

0

). Assume that
\sup_{u,v\in B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1},n\leq\infty}\|f_{n}(\xi_{1}^{n},u,v)\|_{L_{\infty}}<\infty
.

If in addition we know that (

H
minmax

1

) holds and that
d^{\prime}_{n}C_{n}\log(nC_{n})=o(c_{n}n^{1/4})
. Then the bootstrap method is consistent:

\Big\|d_{{\cal F}}\left(\sqrt{n}\big[\hat{\theta}_{n}(Z^{n})-\theta_{Z^{n}}\big],~\sqrt{n}\big[\hat{\theta}_{n}(\xi^{n}_{1:n})-\theta_{n}\big]\Big|\xi^{n}\right)\Big\|_{L_{1}}\rightarrow 0,

where for a process
(Y^{\prime}_{1:n})
we have written

\theta_{Y^{\prime}}:=\inf_{u\in B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}}\sup_{v\in B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}}~\mathbb{E}\big[f_{n}(Y^{{}^{\prime}}_{1},u,v)|X^{n}\big].

Otherwise we suppose that
d^{\prime}_{n}\log(nC_{n})C_{n}=o(n^{1/7})
, and choose
t_{\beta/2}(X^{n})
such that the following holds:

P\left(\sqrt{n}\left|\hat{\theta}_{n}(Z^{n})-\mathbb{E}\left(\hat{\theta}_{n}(Z^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\right|\geq t_{\beta/4}(X^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\leq\beta/4.

We have:

\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}P\left(\sqrt{n}\left|\hat{\theta}_{n}(\xi^{n})-\theta_{X^{n}}\right|\geq t_{\beta/4}(X^{n})+3t_{g,n}^{*,\beta/4}+\delta\right)\leq\beta;

where
t_{g,n}^{*\beta/4}
is chosen such that:
P\left(\sup_{u,v\in B_{d^{\prime}_{n}}^{1}}|N_{u,v}|\geq t_{g,n}^{*,\beta/4}\right)\leq\beta/4
where
(N_{u,v})
is a gaussian process with covariance function
\Sigma^{2}\left((u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})\right):=\rm{Cov}\left(f_{n}(\xi_{1}^{n},u_{1},v_{1}),f_{n}(\xi_{1}^{n},u_{2},v_{2})\right)
.

7
P-value of a Two-Sample Kernel Test

In this subsection, we show how the bootstrap method can be used to obtain consistent p-values for kernel two sample tests. Given two independent i.i.d processes
(X_{i,1}^{n})
and
(X_{i,2}^{n})
taking value in
\mathcal{X}_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
, the goal of two-sample tests is to determine if the observations
(X_{i,1}^{n})
and
(X_{i,2}^{n})
are sampled from the same distribution.
For ease of notations, we designate by
\mu_{n,1}
and
\mu_{n,2}
respectively the distribution of the first sample and second sample; and want test if the null hypothesis holds

(H_{0}^{n}):\mu_{n,1}=\mu_{n,2}

against the alternative

(H_{1}^{n}):\mu_{n,1}\neq\mu_{n,2}.

A popular method to do so are non-parametric kernel two samples tests
[
34
,
26
,
45
,
44
]
.

Let
{\cal F}_{n}
be a class of functions from
\mathcal{X}_{n}
into
\mathbb{R}
. If the two distributions are the same
\mu_{n,1}=\mu_{n,2}
then we have:

\sup_{f\in{\cal F}_{n}}\big|\mathbb{E}(f(X_{1,1}^{n}))-\mathbb{E}(f(X_{1,2}^{n}))\big|=0.

Moreover if
{\cal F}_{n}
is dense in the space of bounded continuous functions then the opposite also holds. The main difficulty therefore consists of choosing the set
{\cal F}_{n}
to be big enough to differentiate between the distributions
\mu_{n,1}
and
\mu_{n,2}
but structured enough that we can estimate of
\sup_{f\in{\cal F}_{n}}\big|\mathbb{E}(f(X_{1,1}^{n}))-\mathbb{E}(f(X_{1,2}^{n}))\big|
. To do so, we choose a reproducing kernel space
\mathcal{H}_{n}
with kernel
K_{n}:\mathcal{X}_{n}\times\mathcal{X}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and set the class of functions
{\cal F}_{n}
to be the unit ball of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Different choices of kernels will lead to various level of power of our test especially for structured or high dimensional data. The goal is to choose the kernel that is the most likely to maximize the power of the test.

Let
\big(K_{\theta_{k}}(\cdot,\cdot)\big)_{k\leq p_{n}}
be a finite set of potential Kernel candidates.
We write for all
i,j\leq n
and for all
k\leq p_{n}

H_{i,j}^{\theta_{k}}:=K_{\theta_{k}}(X^{n}_{j,1},X^{n}_{i,1})+K_{\theta_{k}}(X^{n}_{j,2},X^{n}_{i,2})-K_{\theta_{k}}(X^{n}_{j,1},X^{n}_{i,2})-K_{\theta_{k}}(X^{n}_{j,2},X^{n}_{i,1});

and for all subsets
B\subset\llbracket n\rrbracket
we denote
\hat{M}_{\theta_{k}}(X^{n}_{B}):=\frac{1}{|B|^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in B}H_{i,j}^{\theta_{k}}
.
The idea proposed in
[
34
]
is to select the kernel that gives rise to a test with the highest (estimated) power. This is done by selecting a subset
B_{n}\subset\llbracket n\rrbracket
and maximizing the following quantity
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{B_{n}}:={\rm argmax}_{\theta\in\{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{p_{n}}\}}~p_{\theta}(X^{n}_{B_{n}})
where we have set

p_{\theta}(X^{n}_{B_{n}}):=\frac{\hat{M}_{\theta}(X^{n}_{B_{n}})}{\frac{4}{|B_{n}|^{3}}\sum_{i\in B_{n}}\big[\sum_{j\in B_{n}}H_{i,j}^{\theta}]^{2}-\frac{4}{|B_{n}|^{4}}\Big[\sum_{i,j\leq B_{n}}H_{i,j}^{\theta}\Big]^{2}+\lambda_{n}}

where
(\lambda_{n})
are tuning parameters. Once the kernel is chosen the test statistics is computed on
\llbracket n\rrbracket\setminus B_{n}
the remaining data:
\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in\llbracket n\rrbracket\setminus B_{n}}H^{\hat{\theta}_{n}}_{i,j}
. The fact that the kernel is chosen on a different sample than the test statistics is computed on, means that the conditional limiting distribution of the test statistics, under
H_{0}
, is known to be a chi-square
[
34
]
. Hence one can compute a consistent estimate of the p-value. However under this approach only a portion of the data is used to select the kernel. This could be problematic when dealing with high-dimensional kernels.

We propose a different method that does not require data splitting and uses the bootstrap method to estimate the p-value. The test statistics that we propose is a softmax:

\hat{T}_{n}(X^{n}):=\sum_{k\leq p_{n}}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\leq n}H^{\theta_{k}}_{i,j}~\omega_{k}(X_{1:n}),\qquad{\rm where}~\omega_{k}(X_{1:n}):=\frac{e^{\beta_{n}p_{\theta_{k}}(X^{n})}}{\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq p_{n}}e^{\beta_{n}p_{\theta_{k}^{\prime}}(X^{n})}}~;

and where
(\beta_{n})
are hyper-parameters.
The bigger
\beta_{n}
is the more weight we give to the kernel maximizing
p_{\theta}(X^{n})
.

We note that the distribution of
\hat{T}_{n}
is unknown and depends in an intricate fashion on the set of kernels
\{K_{\theta_{k}},~k\leq p_{n}\}
as well as on
p_{n}
. Therefore to be able to compute the p-value we want to estimate its distribution under
H_{0}
. In this goal, we remark that under the null hypothesis the distribution of
X_{i,1}^{n}
and
X_{i,2}^{n}
are the same which implies that the samples are interchangeable
\left(X_{i,1}^{n},X_{i,2}^{n}\right)\overset{d}{=}\left(X_{i,2}^{n},X_{i,1}^{n}\right)
. It is therefore natural to compare the distribution of
(X^{n}_{i})
to the corresponding randomly permuted process. This is the idea behind permutation tests
[
34
]
. In general, for an i.i.d random process
(\tilde{X}_{i})
taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we define the process
(\tilde{X}^{M}_{i})
obtained by randomly permuting the observations
\tilde{X}_{i,1}
and
\tilde{X}_{i,2}
:

\tilde{X}^{M}_{i}:=\begin{cases}\tilde{X}_{i}~{\rm with~probability~}0.5\\
(\tilde{X}_{i,2},\tilde{X}_{i,1})^{T}~{\rm with~probability~}0.5.\end{cases}

We note that this permuted process has coordinates with identically distributed coordinates
\tilde{X}^{M}_{1,1}\overset{(a)}{=}\tilde{X}^{M}_{1,2}
. Moreover, we have
d_{W}\left(\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{1}^{M}\right)\leq d_{W}\left(\tilde{X}_{1,1},\tilde{X}_{1,2}\right)
and if the distribution of
(\tilde{X}_{i})
are already in
H_{0}
then its distribution is left invariant by those permutations. We show that the bootstrap method allows us to estimate the p-value consistently even when
p_{n}
grows exponentially fast (proof in
Section

I.1
).

Proposition 6
.

Let
(X_{i}^{n}):=\big((X^{n}_{i,1},X^{n}_{i,2})\big)
be a triangular array of i.i.d processes. Let
\{K_{\theta_{k}},~k\leq p_{n}\}
be a sequence of positive definite continuous kernels. We suppose that

\max_{k\leq p_{n}}\rm{tr}(K_{\theta_{k}})<\infty;\quad\frac{\beta_{n}\log(p_{n})D_{n}^{4}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}=o({n}^{1/6});

where we shorthanded
D_{n}:=\max\left(\big\|\sup_{k\leq p_{n}}K_{\theta_{k}}(X_{1,1}^{M},X_{1,1}^{M})\big\|_{L_{120}},~1\right)
. Let
(Y_{i}^{n})
be an independent copy of
(X_{i}^{n})
and
(Z^{n}_{i})
be bootstrap samples of
(X_{i}^{n})
. We have:

\Big\|d_{{\cal F}}\Big(n\hat{T}_{n}(Z^{M}_{1:n}),n\hat{T}_{n}(Y^{M}_{1:n})\mid X^{n}\Big)\Big\|_{L_{1}}\rightarrow 0.

8
Empirical Risk of Smooth Stacked Ensemble Estimator

A ubiquitous and popular approach for model selection and ensembling in machine learning practice is known as stacking
[
46
,
8
,
43
]
. Given a set of trained
base estimators

\{\hat{\theta}^{1},\dots,\hat{\theta}^{p_{n}}\}
, for example representing a fitted neural network, a random forest and a nearest-neighbour estimator, we call the
smooth-stacked estimator
the linear ensemble of those estimators
\{\hat{\theta}^{k}\}
weighted by coefficients that are related to the out-of-sample risk of each estimator. An important question: if we use all the samples to estimate the weights of the ensemble, then can we construct confidence intervals on the risk of the ensemble estimator?

The most straightforward version of stacking is to put all the weight on the model with the smallest out-of-sample risk. Other approaches proposed in practice are to fit a linear regression model using the outputs of each model as an input co-variate to the linear model and using the learned coefficients as coefficients on the ensemble
[
43
]
.

In this subsection, we propose a smooth version of stacking that adds stability to the chosen ensemble, while putting most weight on the best performing model. This ensemble can be viewed as a regularized instance of the linear regression stacking approach where an entropic regularizer is added to the square loss objective. This regularization adds smoothness and stability to the chosen ensemble and allows us to show that the distribution of the ensemble’s risk can be estimated with the bootstrap, even if the all the data are used to estimate the weights or fit the base models.

Let
(X_{i}^{n})
be a triangular array of i.i.d observations taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
; and let
(m_{n})
be an increasing sequence. Define
\mathcal{F}_{n}
as the space of measurable functions from
\times_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}_{n}}
. We estimate
p_{n}
different estimators
\Omega_{n}:=\big\{\hat{\theta}_{n}^{k}(X_{1:m_{n}}^{n}),~k\leq p_{n}\big\}
built on the first
m_{n}
data-points. The loss is measured by a common loss function
\mathcal{L}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}_{n}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and the empirical risk of the
k
-th estimator is computed on all the remaining
n-m_{n}
data points as:

{\cal R}^{k}_{n}(x_{1:n}):=\frac{1}{n-m_{n}}\sum_{u=m_{n}+1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{n}(x_{u},\hat{\theta}^{k}_{n}(x_{1:m_{n}})(x_{u})).

(51)

The smooth-stacked estimator is defined as the following ensemble learner

\hat{\Theta}_{n}(x_{1:n})=\sum_{k\leq p_{n}}\hat{\theta}^{k}_{n}(x_{1:m_{n}})\frac{e^{-\beta_{n}{\cal R}^{k}_{n}(x_{1:n})}}{\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq p_{n}}e^{-\beta_{n}{\cal R}^{k^{\prime}}_{n}(x_{1:n})}}.

(52)

We denote the empirical risk of an estimator
\Theta
as

{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\Theta}(x_{1:n}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n-m_{n}}}\sum_{i=m_{n}+1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{n}\big(x_{i},\Theta(x_{i})\big).

(53)

Let
(Z^{n})
be a bootstrap sample of
(X^{n}_{i})_{i\geq m_{n}}
. We show that the bootstrap method is systematically consistent if and only if
\beta_{n}=o(\sqrt{n-m_{n}})
.
For simplicity we suppose that the estimators
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{k}
have bounded coordinates; and that the loss function
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is smooth, and have bounded partial derivatives in its second argument.

We write the set of all convex combinations of the estimators:

\Omega\left(\left\{\theta_{p},\,p\leq p_{n}\right\}\right):=\left\{\sum_{p\leq p_{n}}\omega_{p}\theta_{p}\mid\omega_{p}\geq 0~{\rm and}~\sum_{p\leq p_{n}}\omega_{p}=1\right\}

(54)

and introduce the following notations:

\begin{split}T_{n}:=~&\sup_{\ell\leq d^{\prime}_{n}}\left\|\sup_{p\leq p_{n}}\left|\hat{\theta}^{p}_{n,\ell}(X_{1:m_{n}}^{n})\right|\right\|_{L_{\infty}}\lor 1,\\
L_{n}:=~&\left\|\sup_{p\leq p_{n}}\left|\mathcal{L}_{n}\left(X_{n}^{n},\hat{\theta}^{p}_{n}(X^{n}_{1:m_{n}})(X_{n}^{n})\right)\right|\right\|_{L_{\infty}}\lor~\sup_{\ell\leq d^{\prime}_{n}}\left\|\sup_{\theta\in\Omega\left(\left\{\hat{\theta}_{p}(X_{1:m_{n}})~p\leq p_{n}\right\}\right)}\partial_{2,\ell}\left|\mathcal{L}_{n}(X_{n}^{n},\theta(X_{n}^{n}))\right|\right\|_{L_{\infty}}\lor 1,\end{split}

where by
\partial_{2,\ell}\mathcal{L}_{n}(x,y)
we designate
\partial_{y_{l}}\mathcal{L}_{n}(x,y)
. We show that if the following hypothesis (

H
1

stacked

) holds then the bootstrap method is asymptotically consistent (proof in
Appendix

J
).

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{n}}{\sqrt{n-m_{n}}d^{\prime}_{n}}L_{n}T_{n}~e^{\frac{\beta_{n}}{n-m_{n}}L_{n}}\longrightarrow 0.

(
H_{1}^{\rm stacked}
)

Proposition 7
.

Choose
(m_{n})
,
(\beta_{n})
and
(p_{n})
be increasing sequences.
Let
(X_{i}^{n})
be a triangular array of i.i.d observations taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
. Set
(\mathcal{L}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R})
to be a sequence of smooth loss functions. Let
(Z_{i}^{n})
and
(Y_{i}^{n})
be respectively a bootstrap sample and an independent copy of
(X_{m_{n}+1}^{n},\dots,X_{n}^{n})
.
Suppose that the hypothesis (

H
1

stacked

) holds then we have:

\begin{split}\Big\|d_{{\cal F}}\Big(&{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Z^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})-\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Z^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})\big|\hat{\Theta}_{n}\big],~{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Y^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})-\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Y^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})\big|\hat{\Theta}_{n}\big]\mid X^{n}\Big)\Big\|_{L_{1}}\rightarrow 0;\end{split}

where we have shorthanded
\hat{\Theta}_{n}:=\hat{\Theta}_{n}(X^{n})
.
Therefore if we choose
t_{n,\alpha}(X^{n})
to be such that:

\begin{split}P\left(\left|{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Z^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})-\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Z^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})\big|\hat{\Theta}_{n}\big]\right|\geq t_{n,\alpha}(X^{n})\mid X^{n}\right)\leq\alpha\end{split}

then the following holds

\begin{split}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}P\left(\left|{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Y^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})-\mathbb{E}\big[{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Y^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})\big|\hat{\Theta}_{n}\big]\right|\geq t_{n,\alpha}(X^{n})\mid\hat{\Theta}_{n}\right)\leq\alpha\end{split}

If
\beta_{n}
grows proportionally to
\beta_{n}\propto\sqrt{n-m_{n}}
then the bootstrap method is not a systematically consistent estimator of the risk of the smooth stacked estimator. We present a simple example illustrating this.

Example 8.1
.

Let
(X_{i})
be a process of i.i.d random variables taking value in
\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
X_{1}\sim N(0,1)
. We choose
m_{n}=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
and
\beta_{n}=\sqrt{n}
and define the estimators
(\hat{\theta}_{n}^{1},\hat{\theta}_{n}^{2})
as constantly equal to

\hat{\theta}_{n}^{1}(X_{1:n}):=1~\quad{\rm and}~\quad\hat{\theta}_{n}^{2}(X_{1:n}):=-1.

We shorthand by
\hat{\Theta}_{n}
the corresponding stacked estimator. We choose the loss function
\mathcal{L}
to be the square loss
\mathcal{L}(x,\theta):=(x-\theta)^{2}
. Let
(Z_{1},Z_{2})\sim N(0,\begin{bmatrix}4&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix})
be a Gaussian vector.
Then the asymptotic centered distribution of the empirical loss is
Z_{1}+Z_{2}{\rm tanh}(4Z_{2})
.
However the asymptotic distribution of the bootstrap empirical loss is

{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Z^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})-\mathbb{E}({\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}}(Z^{n}_{m_{n}+1:n})\big|\hat{\Theta}_{n})\xrightarrow{d}Z_{1}+Z_{2}{\rm tanh}\Big(4Z_{2}+4\sqrt{n}\bar{X}_{m_{n}+1:n}\Big).

Therefore the bootstrap method is not asymptotically consistent.

As
example

8.1
just demonstrated the bootstrap method is not in general consistent for the empirical risk of the stacked estimator. One of the reason for this is the dependence between the weights of the stacked model and the bootstrap samples
Z^{n}
. We therefore slightly adapt the bootstrap method by bootstrapping both the weights and the observations on which the loss is estimated. We establish the limiting distribution of this boostrap estimator as long as
\beta_{n}=O(\sqrt{n-m_{n}})
.

For ease of notations, we denote

\begin{split}&L_{n}^{*}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\leq 3\\
l_{1:i}\leq d^{\prime}_{n}\end{subarray}}\left\|\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq p_{n}\end{subarray}}\left|\partial^{i}_{2,l_{1:i}}\mathcal{L}_{n}\left(X^{n}_{n},\hat{\theta}^{p}_{n}(X^{n}_{1:m_{n}})(X^{n}_{n})\right)\right|\right\|^{1/i}_{L_{\infty}}\lor 1.\end{split}

We establish the limiting distribution of our bootstrap estimate under the following hypothesis:

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{n}d^{\prime}_{n}\log(p_{n})^{2/3}}{{(n-m_{n})}^{2/3}}L_{n}T_{n}\longrightarrow 0

(
H_{1}^{\rm st~bis}
)

Proposition 8
.

Choose
(m_{n})
,
(\beta_{n})
and
(p_{n})
be increasing sequences.
Let
(X_{i}^{n})
be a triangular array of i.i.d observations taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}
. Set
(\mathcal{L}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{n}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R})
to be a sequence of smooth loss functions verifying condition
(H_{1}^{\rm st~bis})
.
Let
(Z_{i}^{n,1})
and
(Z_{i}^{n,2})
be independent bootstrap samples; and
(Y_{i}^{n,1})
and
(Y_{i}^{n,2})
be independent copies of
(X_{m_{n}+1:n}^{n})
.

Then we have:

\begin{split}\Big\|d_{{\cal F}}\Big(&{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}^{Z^{n,2}}}(Z_{1:n-m_{n}}^{n,1})-\mathbb{E}\left({\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}^{Z^{n,2}}}(Z_{1:n-m_{n}}^{n,1})\mid\hat{\Theta}_{n}^{Z^{n,2}},X^{n}\right),\\
&\quad{\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}^{{}^{\prime}}}(Y_{1:n-m_{n}}^{n,1})-\mathbb{E}\left({\cal R}^{{\rm s}}_{\hat{\Theta}_{n}^{{}^{\prime}}}(Y_{1:n-m_{n}}^{n,1})\Big|\hat{\Theta}_{n}^{{}^{\prime}}\right)\mid X^{n}\Big)\Big\|_{L_{1}}\rightarrow 0;\end{split}

where we have set
\hat{\Theta}_{n}^{Z^{n,2}}:=\hat{\Theta}_{n}(X^{n}_{1:m_{n}}Z_{1:n-m_{n}}^{n,2})
and defined

\hat{\Theta}_{n}^{{}^{\prime}}:=\sum_{p\leq p_{n}}\hat{\theta}_{n}^{p}(X_{1:m_{n}}^{n})\frac{e^{-\beta_{n}\big[{\cal R}_{n}^{p}(X_{1:m_{n}}^{n}Y_{1:n-m_{n}}^{n,2})+\tilde{}{\cal R}_{n}^{p}(X^{n})\big]}}{\sum_{p^{\prime}\leq p_{n}}e^{-\beta_{n}\big[{\cal R}_{n}^{p^{\prime}}(X_{1:m_{n}}^{n}Y_{1:n-m_{n}}^{n,2})+\tilde{}{\cal R}_{n}^{p^{\prime}}(X^{n})\big]}};

where we wrote
\tilde{}{\cal R}_{n}^{p}(X^{n}):={\cal R}_{n}^{p}(X^{n})-\sqrt{n-m_{n}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{L}_{n}(X_{n}^{n},\theta_{p}^{n}(X_{1:m_{n}}^{n}))\big|\theta_{p}^{n}(X_{1:m_{n}}^{n})\big]
.

We notice that this implies that the bootstrap method is in general not consistent if
\beta_{n}=o(\sqrt{n-m_{n}})
, as it is illustrated in
example

8.1
.

Appendix A
Preliminary Lemmas and Notation

If a function
f
is three-times differentiable then we let:

\displaystyle\partial_{i,k}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i,k}}f(x_{1:n})

\displaystyle\partial_{i}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle(\partial_{i,1}f(x_{1:n}),\dots,\partial_{i,d}f(x_{1:n}))^{\top}

(55)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{i,k_{1:2}}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i,k_{1}}}\partial_{x_{i,k_{2}}}f(x_{1:n})

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{i}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle\left(\partial^{2}_{i,k_{1:2}}f(x_{1:n})\right)_{k_{1},k_{2}\leq d}

(56)

\displaystyle\partial^{3}_{i,k_{1:3}}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i,k_{1}}}\partial_{x_{i,k_{2}}}\partial_{x_{i,k_{3}}}f(x_{1:n})

\displaystyle\partial_{i}^{3}f(x_{1:n})~:=~

\displaystyle\left(\partial^{3}_{i,k_{1:3}}f(x_{1:n})\right)_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\leq d}

(57)

A.1
Preliminary results

Lemma 8
.

Let
(\tilde{X}^{n}_{i})
be an array of martingale differences taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{p_{n}}
. Suppose that
\left\|\max_{k\leq p_{n}}\tilde{X}^{n}_{1,k}\right\|_{L_{p}}<\infty
where
p\geq 3
. Then there exists a constant
C_{p}
, that does not depend on the distribution of
(\tilde{X}_{i}^{n})
, such that

\left\|\max_{k\leq p_{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}\tilde{X}^{n}_{i,k}\right\|_{L_{p}}\leq\left\|\max_{k\leq p_{n}}\tilde{X}^{n}_{i,k}\right\|_{L_{p}}\left(1+C_{p}\left(\log(p_{n})+\frac{\log(p_{n})^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)

(58)

Thus if
\log(p_{n})=o(n^{1/4})
then

\left\|\max_{k\leq p_{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\leq n}\tilde{X}^{n}_{i,k}\right\|_{L_{p}}=O\left(\log(p_{n})\left\|\sup_{k\leq p_{n}}\tilde{X}^{n}_{i,k}\right\|_{L_{p}}\right).

(59)

Moreover let
(X^{n}_{i})
be a triangular array of i.i.d process and
(g_{k,n})
be sequences of measurable functions, for each
k\in[p]
. Then:

\left\|\max_{k\leq p_{n}}g_{k,n}\left(X^{n}\right)\right\|_{L_{p}}=O\left(\log(p_{n})\,\sqrt{n}\,\left\|\sup_{k\leq p_{n}}\left|g_{k,n}\left(X^{n}\right)-g_{k,n}\left(X^{n,i}\right)\right|\right\|_{L_{p}}\right),

(60)

where we have defined
X^{n,i}_{j}:=\begin{cases}X^{n}_{j},&{\rm if}~~j\neq i\\
X^{\prime}_{i},&{\rm if}~~i=j\end{cases}
with
(X_{i}^{\prime})
being an independent copy of
(X^{n}_{i})
.

See
Section

L.1
for proof of
Lemma

8
.

Lemma 9
.

For any set of random variables
U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{t\leq m}U_{t}\right)^{d}\right]\leq\left(\sum_{t\leq m}\|U_{t}\|_{L_{d}}\right)^{d}

(61)

Proof.

By expanding the polynomial, applying a repeated version of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and collapsing the polynomial again, we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{t\leq m}U_{t}\right)^{d}\right]=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t_{1:d}\leq m}\prod_{\ell=1}^{d}U_{t_{\ell}}\right]\leq\sum_{t_{1:d}\leq m}\prod_{\ell=1}^{d}\|U_{t_{\ell}}\|_{L_{d}}\leq\left(\sum_{t\leq m}\|U_{t}\|_{L_{d}}\right)^{d}

(62)

∎

Lemma 10
.

The distribution distance
d_{{\cal F}}
satisfies the triangle inequality.

Proof.

For any three random variables
X,Y,Z
:

\displaystyle d_{\cal F}(X,Z):=~

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in{\cal F}}\mathbb{E}[h(X)]-\mathbb{E}[h(Z)]

(63)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in{\cal F}}\mathbb{E}[h(X)]-\mathbb{E}[h(Y)]+\mathbb{E}[h(Y)]-\mathbb{E}[h(Z)]

(64)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in{\cal F}}\mathbb{E}[h(X)]-\mathbb{E}[h(Y)]+\sup_{h\in{\cal F}}\mathbb{E}[h(Y)]-\mathbb{E}[h(Z)]=:d_{\cal F}(X,Y)+d_{\cal F}(Y,Z)

(65)

∎

Lemma 11
.

The distribution distance
d_{{\cal F}}
is translation invariant: For all random variables
X
and
Y
and all constant
z
we have

d_{\mathcal{F}}\left(X,Y\right)=d_{\mathcal{F}}\left(X-z,Y-z\right)

Proof.

For all
h\in\mathcal{F}
define
h_{z}x\rightarrow h(x-z)
. We have:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}{h(X-z)-h(Y-z)}}={\mathbb{E}{h_{z}(X)-h_{z}(Y)}}\leq d_{{\cal F}}\left(X,Y\right).

(66)

As this holds for all
h\in{\cal F}
it implies that

\displaystyle d_{{\cal F}}\left(X-z,Y-z\right)\leq d_{{\cal F}}\left(X,Y\right).

(67)

The reverse inequality is proved in exactly the same fashion.
∎

Lemma 12
.

Let
p_{1},p_{2}
be two distributions that are uniformly continuous with respect to a measure
\mu
. Then the following holds:

1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-KL(p_{2},p_{1})}\geq\|p_{1}(\cdot)-p_{2}(\cdot)\|_{TV}

Proof.

We denote
f_{1},f_{2}
the Radon-Nikodym densities of respectively
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
with respect to
\mu
.
By the Cauchy-Swartz inequality we have:

\displaystyle\left(\int\sqrt{f_{1}(x)f_{2}(X)}d\mu(x)\right)^{2}

(68)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int\sqrt{\min(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(x))\max(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(X))}d\mu(x)\right)^{2}

(69)

\displaystyle\leq{\int{\min(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(x))d\mu(x)\int\max(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(X))d\mu(x)}}

(70)

\displaystyle\leq{\int{\min(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(x))d\mu(x)\left(2-\int\min(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(X))\right)d\mu(x)}}

(71)

\displaystyle\leq 2(1-\|p_{1}(\cdot)-p_{2}(\cdot)\|_{TV}).

(72)

Moreover by another application of Cauchy-Swartz we know that

\displaystyle\left(\int\sqrt{f_{1}(x)f_{2}(X)}d\mu(x)\right)^{2}

(73)

\displaystyle=e^{\log\left(\left(\int\sqrt{f_{1}(x)f_{2}(X)}d\mu(x)\right)^{2}\right)}

(74)

\displaystyle=e^{2\log\left({\int\frac{\sqrt{f_{1}(x)}}{\sqrt{f_{2}(x)}}{f_{2}(X)}d\mu(x)}\right)}

(75)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-KL(p_{2},p_{1})}

(76)

Therefore by combining those two inequalities we obtain that:

1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-KL(p_{2},p_{1})}\geq\|p_{1}(\cdot)-p_{2}(\cdot)\|_{TV}

∎

Appendix B
Extensions and Variations of Main Theorem
